## 2026-10-10: M1 back end, wave 1

Goal: log the gaps that wave 1 of the M1 back end (MIR contracts, the runtime ABI, code generation for every MIR form, numbers and lists) settled in code, and the user's decision on correctly rounded math after its verification failed.

- **D-555 Objects with an escaped scan count**: An object with more than 254 leading uniform fields has the scan count 255, the escape value of D-279. A constructor keeps its fields where its layout puts them, and freeing, equality, hashing and debug text read them from the layout table. A tuple holds its arity as a tagged integer at offset 8, then its elements. A closure, dictionary or descriptor keeps its first field, the code word, type descriptor or nominal descriptor, at offset 8, holds the number of the fields after the count as a tagged integer at offset 16, then those fields. Static dictionaries and descriptors have the same shape as allocated ones. Why: D-279 promises an escape value but not where the real count goes. A constructor's layout already knows its fields. With the first field at offset 8, indirect calls (D-453) and readers of a dictionary's descriptor never check for the escape, and freeing skips the tagged count word like any tagged word. A front-end limit on field counts with a new diagnostic was rejected, because it would refuse valid programs. Refines D-279, D-524.
- **D-556 Location of an equality panic on functions**: `cheby_rt_equal` takes the source location of the `==` or `!=` as a fourth argument: `bool (ctx*, u64 a, u64 b, u64 location)`, where `location` is a static `String`, or 0 when unknown. MIR's `RuntimeFn::Equal` takes it as a third, borrowed operand, and the MIR validator rejects anything there but a string constant. A structural `==` or `!=` that reaches a function value panics with `EqualityOnFunction` (D-233) at that location. Only such a panic raised inside a kernel, such as a map kernel hashing or comparing its keys, keeps `<unknown location>`, because no Cheby location exists there. This changes the signature of `cheby_rt_equal` that D-520 lists. Why: §11.6 gives every panic report the location where the panic started, D-449 allows `<unknown location>` only when none is known, and the location of a written `==` is known at compile time. A three-state result that generated code turns into a located panic was rejected, because it adds a branch and a second panic path at every `==`. Refines D-520.
- **D-557 Hex digits of `\u{…}` in debug text**: Debug text writes the `\u{…}` escape of a control character (D-531), a character of Unicode's general category Cc, in lower-case hex without leading zeros, such as `\u{0}`, `\u{1b}` and `\u{7f}`. Why: D-531 fixes which characters are escaped but not the digits, and §2.5 accepts 1 to 6 digits in either case. Debug text must be deterministic and the same on every target, and the shortest lower-case form is also Rust's. Refines D-531.
- **D-558 `-0` on unsigned integer types**: The optional `-` of D-312 is accepted by `parse` and `parse_base` of every integer type, the unsigned ones included, and only the value's range is checked. So `u8::parse("-0")` and `u8::parse("-00")` are `Ok(0)`, and `u8::parse("-1")` is `Err(Nil)`. The JS runtime (M4) must match. Why: D-312 checks the value's range, not whether a sign is present, and `-0` is the value 0, which every integer type holds. Rejecting the sign on unsigned types would add a type-specific rule to a grammar written once for all integer types. Refines D-312.
- **D-559 Type keys**: A type's key for hashing is SipHash-1-3 with a zero key over the UTF-8 bytes of its module path, written with `::` as in `app::colors` and empty for builtin types, then one `0xff` byte, then the bytes of the type's bare name, without type arguments. Builtin types such as `Option`, `Result`, `Order`, `Bool` and `Nil` use the empty path and their name. Hashing a constructor value hashes its type key and its tag, equality compares them, and neither reads a layout id. `cheby_mir::type_key` and `cheby_runtime::layout::type_key` compute the same function. Why: D-538 says that a constructor's hash uses its module path and name, but not how they are combined. The `0xff` byte never occurs in UTF-8, so a path and a name cannot run together, and one formula for user and builtin types keeps hashes independent of layout ids (D-264). Refines D-538.
- **D-560 Symbols of tuple functions and `dyn` forwarders**: The code of a built-in tuple interface function (D-167) is `cb$tfn$<module path>$<interface function>$<arity>`, and the forwarding entry of an interface function in `dyn` dictionaries is `cb$dynfwd$<module path>$<interface function>`, where the module path is the interface function's. This adds the kinds `tfn` and `dynfwd` to D-456 and D-523. Why: the plan's MIR has these generated functions, which depend only on the interface function and the arity, but D-456 and D-523 name no kind for them. A kind of their own keeps the kind-first rule, which rules out collisions. Extends D-456, D-523.
- **D-561 Defined once per JIT**: D-523's rule that a static is defined once per process holds per JIT, the program image of one build or one REPL session, and separate JITs in one process define their own statics. A static dictionary, descriptor or function value is defined by the first unit or batch of a JIT that names its symbol, and later units and batches reuse that object without comparing its contents. Code that depends only on its key follows the same rule: value shims, built-in tuple functions, `dyn` forwarders and trampolines. A later declaration of one with the same signature reuses the first code, and any other function declared twice is an internal compiler error. Why: each JIT owns its memory and frees it when dropped, so a static cannot be shared across JITs safely. Several units may need the same shim or trampoline, such as two modules that use one function as a value, and defining a symbol twice is an error in Cranelift's JIT. Refines D-523.
- **D-562 Raw constructor fields**: A raw constructor field fills its whole 8-byte slot. `Int`, `I64`, `U64` and `Float` are stored as they are, `I8`, `I16` and `I32` are sign-extended to 64 bits, `U8`, `U16`, `U32` and `Bool` are zero-extended, and an `F32` keeps its bits in the low 32 bits with the high 32 bits zero. Why: the plan gives every raw field an 8-byte slot but not how a narrower value fills it. A fully defined slot makes a read of any width correct and keeps frozen constants (D-539) and future object images deterministic (D-264). Refines D-454.
- **D-563 List objects**: A non-empty list is an RRB tree (D-039) of uniform words with branching factor 32, made of three kinds of objects. A root, layout id 26, is the list value and a view: the header, then the tree, the view's offset into it and its length, the last two raw. A heap root always has a length above 0, and the empty list is always the static `cheby_rt_static_empty_list`. A leaf, id 27, holds a raw word with the length in its low 32 bits and the capacity in its high 32 bits, then `capacity` element slots, at most 32, and a unique leaf doubles its capacity when it grows. A branch, id 28, holds a raw word with the child count in its low 32 bits and the height in its high 32 bits, where leaves have height 0, then 32 child slots, then 32 cumulative sizes. Every branch keeps its size table, so balanced and relaxed nodes are one kind. Ids 29 to 31 stay unused. Concatenation merges the trees along the seam (Bagwell and Rompf) and repacks a run of nodes next to the seam when it uses more than 2 nodes beyond the fewest that could hold its slots. A kernel never updates an argument in place when another argument is the same object, as in `list::append(xs, xs)`. `list::prepend` at count one updates a unique left edge in place, so unique prepends allocate amortized O(1). `cheby_rt_list_get` with an index out of range is a fatal error, because lowering checks the length first, and `cheby_rt_list_slice` clamps its bounds like `list::slice`. Why: D-524 reserves ids 26 to 31 for list nodes but leaves their shape open. These choices give D-053's O(1) views and the costs that `list.md` requires (D-300), and the alias rule keeps the in-place updates of D-525 sound when a kernel gets the same object twice. Refines D-524.
- **D-564 Accounting domains**: The runtime counts live objects and bytes per accounting domain, not in one process-wide counter. Each JIT has its own domain, which every entry of its build charges: constants, `main` and tests. Running an entry makes the JIT's domain the thread's current one, and allocation, freeing and `forget` charge the current domain. `forget` takes a frozen object out of its domain exactly once. The leak check of D-458 reads the domain's live-object count after `main` returns, as an absolute number, since constants frozen before `main` (D-539) have already left it. The per-test leak check of `cheby test` (D-541) compares the domain before and after the test, and the memory limit of constant evaluation (D-536) reads the domain's peak bytes during the evaluation. Why: parallel tests and several JITs in one process must not count each other's objects. With one domain per build, only the program's own objects are live at exit, and an absolute count also catches objects leaked by earlier entries of the same build, which a difference would miss. Refines D-458.
- **D-565 CLIF dumps ignore poisoning**: The driver's CLIF dump, which goldens and the determinism check use, always compiles with the default options, with the inline fast paths of count operations. `CHEBY_RT_POISON=1` makes only running code call the checked `cheby_rt_dup` and `cheby_rt_drop`, and never changes a dump. Why: dumps must not depend on the environment (D-264), and the harnesses set `CHEBY_RT_POISON=1` for every `cheby` they start (D-544). Refines D-544.
- **D-566 Which pending tests a strict run requires**: With `CHEBY_PENDING=require`, a failing pending test fails the run only when its milestone is the run's milestone in progress, that milestone is not later than the one `tests/milestone` names, and the allow list does not name the test. These stay report-only even in strict runs: a `skip-run` example's `cheby check` before its milestone (D-445), an example's `::test` trial before its milestone or without its fixture (D-472), and a test of a milestone later than `tests/milestone` that the `CHEBY_MILESTONE` override made pending. So `CHEBY_MILESTONE=M2` requires every M1 test, while M2 tests may still fail. Why: D-544 makes the override require every M1 test and says nothing about the next milestone's tests. The check-only examples 005, 009, 013, 018, 019, 022 and 028 have milestone M2 and must still report E0038 at M1's exit (D-477), so requiring them would make M1's exit check unsatisfiable. The trials that D-445 and D-472 keep pending cannot pass by design, and requiring them would need permanent allow entries. Refines D-544.
- **D-567 Values of the strict-run variables**: `CHEBY_PENDING` accepts `require`, and `report`, empty or unset give D-288's behavior. Any other value is a harness error, and the harness exits with status 2. An empty `CHEBY_MILESTONE` is no override, and a value that names no milestone is a harness error. The file that `CHEBY_PENDING_ALLOW` names is read only in strict runs, and a file that cannot be read is then a harness error. Without the variable, or with it empty, the allow list is empty. In the file, `#` starts a comment that runs to the end of the line, and blank lines are ignored. The pattern is the first whitespace-separated word of a line, and the rest of the line is a free note, such as the reason and the gate. In a pattern, `*` matches any run of characters, `/` and `:` included, `?` matches one character, and every other character matches itself. The summary groups the pending tests as passed, allowed failures with the matching pattern, required failures, and still failing. Why: a typo such as `requre` must not silently make a strict gate lenient. The plan's carried list puts each test's reason and gate on its pattern's line, so the rest of the line has to be ignored. Refines D-544.
- **D-568 Correctly rounded math in Rust**: `float::pow`, `exp`, `ln`, `sin`, `cos`, `tan` and `atan2` (D-396) are implemented in Rust in the runtime, with double-double evaluation and Ziv's rounding test. Each one is registered only after it passes D-553's verification, with the same reference values, inputs and hard cases. Task W4-D implements them and W5-D finishes what is left. The build needs no C compiler. Why: the user decided it after none of the seven passed D-553's verification with `pxfm` 0.1.30, the only pure-Rust CORE-MATH port found. It gave wrong special values for `pow` and `atan2`, such as `-0.0` for `pow(-0.0, 3.141592653589793)`, gross errors on subnormal results of `exp`, and 1-ulp misroundings on hard cases. Vendoring CORE-MATH's C sources was rejected, because it adds a C compiler to the build, and shipping M1 without the seven functions was rejected, because it leaves tier 1 incomplete. Refines D-553.
