- ~~The Windows CI jobs still run the JIT and runtime unit tests, the driver tests that compile and run examples, and the check-pass UI tests that evaluate constants through the JIT. None has run on Windows, so D-545 is unverified for them. W7-A or the user decides whether to gate them with `cfg(not(windows))`.~~ → D-641.
- Optional: a dedicated "parenthesize the record" help for the two first-subject shapes of §5.9.6, which get generic E0013 errors (D-639).
- Optional: W4-E's `path_uses` runs twice, a speed refactor.

## 2026-10-11: M1 exit

Goal: log the decisions of the M1 exit checkpoint (the answer to OQ-P10 from the exit measurements, the Windows test rule, and what the exit fixes settled in code for allocation failures and CLIF translation), stating what the implementation does.

- ~~**D-640 Throughput budget at M1 exit**: M1 checks and generates code at 95k to 97k lines per second per core on `bench/gen` projects of 100k and 1M lines, and at 85k to 88k on projects of 10k lines, and it scales 3.6x to 3.7x at 8 threads. These are task I3's measurements at commit `dc0ef73`, on an Apple M3 with 4 performance and 4 efficiency cores, with a release-built compiler and debug code generation. Until M3's exit, the throughput budget is the measured 95k lines per second per core for projects of 100k lines or more, with a speedup of at least 3.5x at 8 threads. D-247's 100k lines per second per core becomes the target at M3's exit, measured on the same shapes by CI's Linux runner once its bench job reports. The budgets stay reported, not enforced, until M6. D-247's edit-to-run budget is unchanged and stays with OQ-P11: editing one function of the 1M-line project and running it took 0.49 s to 0.53 s against its 500 ms. Why: the user chose the measured baseline. The miss is under 5% at scale. The back end takes 87% of the time, and over 80% of that is inside Cranelift, so M2's smaller code (borrowing and reuse, fewer box and unbox round trips) is the lever, not a new budget. Confirming D-247 unchanged was rejected, because a budget that M1 measurably misses becomes a goal. Deferring to M3 was rejected, because caching does not change clean-build throughput. Refines D-247. Settles OQ-P10.~~ Superseded by D-644.
- **D-641 Tests on Windows before M6**: Until M6, Windows CI builds every test but runs only the tests that execute no generated code, and it does not build the JIT crate's own tests. The test modules of `cheby_jit`, in `src/lib.rs` and the reservation tests in `src/memory.rs`, carry `#[cfg(test)]` and `#[cfg(not(windows))]`, so they are not built on Windows. The reservation tests run no generated code, but the Windows reservation path has never run, so M6 brings it up with the rest. `TODO(M6)` notes mark the Windows-only test code this leaves unbuilt: `process_memory` in `src/tests.rs` and the Windows dev-dependency in `Cargo.toml`. Every other test that builds or runs a JIT carries `#[cfg_attr(windows, ignore = "runs JIT-compiled code; Windows support is M6 (D-545)")]`: 6 tests of `cheby_codegen_clif` that create a Cranelift JIT module, 33 tests of `cheby_driver`, `examples_strip_to_plain` in `cheby_cli`'s `tests/color.rs`, and `generated_projects_check_cleanly` in `bench/gen`. They include every test that runs `cheby check` on a package without front-end errors, because `check` then evaluates the constants with the JIT (D-534). `UiTest::builds_a_jit` in `tests/support/src/ui.rs` decides which UI tests are ignored on Windows: every run test, and every check test whose `.stderr` golden has no `error[CODE]` line with a front-end code. The back-end codes are E0053, E0069 and E0075 to E0078, and a denied warning such as `error[W0002]` counts as front-end. E0069 is also reported by the front end, so counting it ignores a few more tests. The UI harness and `ui_tests_strip_to_plain` in `tests/color.rs` share this rule. The conformance harness ignores every trial on Windows, including the checks of the `skip-run` examples 020, 030 and 031, although 030 and 031 stop in the front end. The REPL suite stays ignored, and `cheby_cli`'s `tests/run.rs` stays unbuilt. Each harness prints the reason "programs do not run on Windows until M6 (D-545)" once. The header comment of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` states this rule. Why: the user chose it. D-545 kept "the unit tests" on Windows, but `cheby check` on a clean package builds a JIT, and the JIT's `VirtualAlloc` path, native symbol lookup off Unix and the entry stacks have never run on Windows. A red Windows job would block every pull request to main. Fixing what fails after the first Windows CI run was rejected, because it needs a push before the exit. Only building on Windows was rejected, because it drops the front-end unit tests that D-545 keeps. Refines D-545.
- **D-642 An allocation request that cannot be served**: In the native runtime, every allocation request that cannot be served ends in `cheby_runtime::alloc::out_of_memory(bytes: u64)`: a size that overflows 64 bits or the address space, or a request the system allocator refuses. This covers objects, the text of `String` and `Bytes`, the interpolation buffer, and the result sizes that kernels compute, such as those of `string::repeat`, `pad_start`, `pad_end`, `join` and `bytes::concat`. A size that cannot be computed is passed as `u64::MAX`. During constant evaluation, a request over the memory budget, which includes every size that overflows, ends the evaluation with E0076, like any request over the limit (D-617). Anywhere else, and for a request under the budget that the system refuses during constant evaluation, the running entry panics with the message `out of memory` at `<unknown location>`. So `cheby run` exits with 101, `cheby test` fails the test, the REPL abandons the entry and continues (D-457), and a constant gives E0075 with the note "panicked at <unknown location>: out of memory". Outside any entry, which only the runtime's host-side callers reach, it is a fatal runtime error. The Rust-side buffers of the runtime's walks are scratch buffers (`alloc::scratch`): the worklists of debug printing, equality and hashing, and the table of `list::unique`. Their capacity is counted per thread and added to the domain's live bytes when the budget is checked, and they grow with `try_reserve_exact`, so a refused growth also ends in `out_of_memory`. Kernels whose temporary would be the size of their result write the result object directly: `string::split`, `reverse`, `uppercase`, `lowercase`, `from_code_point_values` and `bytes::from_list`. Debug printing escapes a `String` or `Bytes` in 1 KiB chunks. The runtime holds one 4 MiB reserve per process, set aside again when an entry starts after it was used, and `out_of_memory` frees it before it reports. So even complete exhaustion ends as the panic, not as an abort while the report is written. Why: neither the spec nor the log said what an allocation that cannot be served does at run time. The runtime treated it as a broken invariant and aborted with exit 134 and no diagnostic, which also killed a REPL session. §11.8 makes a panic of every other condition that a program's values cause, and a panic keeps a REPL session and a test run alive. A refusal under the limit is not over the limit in D-617's sense, and E0076's help to raise `memory-limit` would be false for it. Aborting, as Rust's `handle_alloc_error` does, was rejected, because the same call would be a recoverable E0076 in a constant but a silent process death in a REPL entry or a test. Refines D-449, D-457, D-617.
- **D-643 Code generation binds MIR locals to CLIF values directly**: The CLIF translator of `cheby_codegen_clif` uses no `cranelift_frontend` `Variable`s. MIR is SSA with block parameters, so each MIR local is bound to one CLIF value where it is defined, and CLIF block n is MIR block n, with its parameters created up front. Blocks are translated in reverse postorder from `bb0`, computed without recursion. A MIR block that `bb0` does not reach is not translated and is not in the CLIF. A local defined twice, defined by a value of another type, or used before its definition in that order is an internal compiler error. A constant `Int` or `I64` that fits in 63 bits, or a constant `U64` up to 2⁶² − 1, is boxed at compile time to its tagged word (D-521), with no range check and no call of `cheby_rt_box_i64` or `cheby_rt_box_u64`. The panic block of each kind and location is found through a hash map, and panic blocks are emitted in the order they were first needed, as before. Generated code behaves as before, but the CLIF text changes: it has no value aliases or temporary block parameters, fewer imports and some `.i64` suffixes. The golden `crates/cheby_driver/tests/golden/001_hello_world.clif` was blessed again, and the CLIF dumps of D-620 stay identical across thread counts and paths. A list literal of constant elements adds no CLIF block per element and compiles in linear time: 100k elements take 0.75 s under `cheby run` and 0.69 s under `cheby check` with a release compiler. A literal of computed elements adds a fixed number of blocks per element, but it still compiles in superlinear time inside regalloc2. Why: Cranelift's SSA construction costs memory per variable and block, which made a list literal of n elements quadratic and ran out of memory at 100k computed elements. MIR's validator already guarantees one definition per local and uses dominated by it, which is what that construction rebuilds. Keeping `Variable`s only for locals that live across blocks was rejected, because it still goes quadratic when each element branches. Chunking the literal in MIR was rejected, because it does not touch the cause.
- **D-644 Throughput budget at M1 exit, after D-643**: D-247's throughput budget holds at M1 and stays as written: a clean debug build checks and generates code for at least 100k lines per second per core and scales with the number of cores, with no numeric floor for the scaling. After D-643, two runs of `cargo xtask bench-compile` at commit `731dac1`, on the Apple M3 of D-640, measured 104k to 107k lines per second per core for checking with code generation on `bench/gen` projects of 100k and 1M lines, 89k to 94k on projects of 10k lines, and 117k to 118k on the 10k-line `generic` shape. On 8 threads it scales 3.5x to 3.7x on projects of 100k lines or more, except 3.3x in both runs on the 100k-line `large` project, whose 6 modules give the parallel passes little to split, and 2.6x to 3.0x on projects of 10k lines. Editing one function of the 1M-line `wide` project and running it took 0.484 s and 0.489 s, against D-247's 500 ms, which stays with OQ-P11. The budgets stay reported, not enforced, until M6, and M3's exit measures them again on CI's Linux runner. Why: the user chose to confirm D-247 once the measurements after D-643 met it. D-640's 95k baseline was measured before D-643, when code generation still spent time in Cranelift's SSA construction. A floor of 3.5x at 8 threads was rejected, because the 6-module project misses it and D-247 asks only for scaling, and a floor of 3.3x was rejected, because one small project would set it. Supersedes D-640. Settles OQ-P10.

A tuple literal with n computed fields compiles in quadratic time, because MIR boxes every field through a branch before the single `alloc tuple/n`. The options for M2 are splitting very large functions, regalloc2 or Cranelift options, and boxing each field after the `alloc`.~~ → D-720.

## 2026-10-11: M2 design

Goal: settle M2's prerequisites (the tier-2 stdlib, OQ-P7 and OQ-P8) and every gap the M2 survey found, ahead of the vertical slice (005 on the JIT with fibers and channels) and the user's checkpoint review.

- **D-645 The tier-2 module list**: Tier 2 has six modules: `std::fiber`, `std::channel`, `std::selector`, `std::duration`, `std::random` and `std::runtime`. A tier-2 table in `docs/stdlib/README.md` lists them, and the tier-2 table of `docs/plan/stdlib.md` links them. `random::between` and "seeding" leave the plan's list. Why: the README's tier sentence omits `std::runtime`, which D-175 and the plan need. `random::between` is the function of example 010's own module (D-229), and seeding is `random::from_seed` (D-653). Refines D-276, D-294.
- **D-646 Names of `std::fiber`, `std::channel` and `std::selector`**: The tier-2 names are `fiber::scope`, `spawn`, `spawn_with_stack`, `spawn_detached`, `join`, `timeout`, `message` and `show`, with the types `Scope`, `Fiber<T>`, `Panic` and `Timeout { TimedOut, Panicked(Panic) }`; `channel::new`, `send` and `receive`, with `Sender<T>` and `Receiver<T>`; and `selector::new`, `receive`, `after` and `select`, with `Selector<E>`. The signatures are those of `docs/stdlib/fiber.md`, `channel.md` and `selector.md`. `spawn`'s scope parameter is named `owner` and `message`'s parameter `failure`, because a parameter cannot share its name with a function of its module (D-051, E0044) and `panic` is a keyword. Why: every name the examples use is kept, so no example changes. The design's probe checks all seven M2 examples against these exact signatures. Refines D-174, D-199.
- **D-647 Tier-2 handles are external types of their modules**: `Sender<T>` and `Receiver<T>` (`std::channel`), `Scope` and `Fiber<T>` (`std::fiber`) and `Selector<E>` (`std::selector`) are declared without a body, so they are external types (§12.5.3, D-491) with drop functions (D-101). They print as `<Sender>`, `<Receiver>`, `<Scope>`, `<Fiber>` and `<Selector>`. `==` and hashing panic on them (D-189, D-233), W0011 warns about comparing types that contain them (D-518), and E0068 rejects them in constants. `Selector` is a handle because it holds receivers and functions. §3.2's row that makes `Sender` and `Receiver` built-in types homed in `std::channel` is replaced: they are ordinary types of `std::channel`. On JS, every one of these handles is reference-counted like `Sender` and `Receiver`, and so is every value that can hold one. M2 runs the drop functions of std's handles; user external types get theirs with OQ-P9 in M3 (D-703). Why: external types already have every behavior D-233 asks of handles, with no new compiler code, and the existing external-type rule meets D-139's and D-511's "channels and fibers join the check with the tier-2 library". Built-in types would need changes to `cheby_iface`, the constant check and the warnings for the same observable result. D-100 names only `Sender` and `Receiver`, but on JS a selector that holds the last `Receiver` must release it when `select` returns, as on native. Refines D-139, D-233, D-511, D-590. Extends D-100.
- **D-648 `fiber::Panic`'s equality, debug text and `show`**: `Panic` is opaque plain data holding the message of D-473 and the location as the panic report writes it (`path:line:column`, `<unknown location>` or `<builtin>`, D-449). Two panics are `==` when both texts are equal, and they hash accordingly. Its debug text is `Panic { message: "…", location: "…" }`. `fiber::show` makes it satisfy `Show`, with the text of the panic report without the final line ending, so `main` returning `Err(p)` prints the same two lines a panic would and exits with 1. There is no location accessor. Why: examples compare `Result<_, fiber::Panic>` with `==` and debug-print panics (009), and D-416 left the debug text to tier 2. A report-shaped `show` keeps `main`'s error output readable for the four examples whose `main` returns `Result<Nil, fiber::Panic>`. Leaving `Show` out was the minimal alternative, but adding it later would change what those programs print. Refines D-178, D-416, D-473.
- **D-649 `fiber::Timeout`**: The error of `fiber::timeout` is `pub exposed type Timeout { TimedOut, Panicked(Panic) }`, with structural `==`. Why: examples match `fiber::TimedOut` and `fiber::Panicked(p)` and compare with `== Err(fiber::TimedOut)` from other packages, and no example writes the type's name, so the provisional name stays. Settles D-199. Refines D-181.
- **D-650 `Duration` is a Cheby record of seconds and nanoseconds**: `std::duration` declares `Duration` as an opaque record written in Cheby, with an `Int` of whole seconds and an `Int` of nanoseconds that is always from 0 to 999,999,999. The resolution is 1 ns. The seconds are any `Int`, so spans may be negative and reach about ±292 billion years on native and ±285 million years on JS. Arithmetic beyond that panics like `Int` arithmetic (D-025, D-037). The module has no kernels, so every function runs in constants and gives the same result on every target. `==` and hashing are the built-in structural ones, which compare spans because the representation is unique. Debug text is the built-in record form, such as `Duration { seconds: 1, nanoseconds: 500000000 }`. Why: example 019 calls `duration::seconds` in `const` initializers and compares spans with `==`, and a Cheby record gets both with no new rule. A single `Int` of nanoseconds was rejected because on JS it panics past about 104 days (D-037). Coarser units were rejected because tier 3's clock measures in nanoseconds. Unsigned spans were rejected because `a - b` would then need a `Result`. `@std_hooks` for a shorter debug text was rejected: debug output shows fields (D-134), and `show` gives the short text. Refines D-103.
- **D-651 The functions of `std::duration`**: `std::duration` has the constant `zero`; the constructors `nanoseconds`, `microseconds`, `milliseconds`, `seconds`, `minutes` and `hours`, which take an `Int` that may be negative; the readers `to_nanoseconds`, `to_microseconds`, `to_milliseconds` and `to_seconds`, which truncate toward zero like `/`; the operators' functions `add`, `sub` and `neg`; and `compare`, `min`, `max` and `show`. `Duration` satisfies `Add`, `Sub`, `Neg`, `Compare` and `Show`. `show` writes `0s` for zero, and otherwise an optional `-` and the length in the largest of `s`, `ms`, `µs` (U+00B5) and `ns` in which it is at least 1, with its exact decimal fraction and no trailing zeros, such as `1.5s`, `100ms`, `1.001µs` and `-250ms`. There is no `days`, `mul`, `div` or `scale`. Why: the examples need `seconds`, `milliseconds`, `==` and constants, and timeouts and backoff need the rest. A calendar day is not always 24 hours, which tier 3's `std::time` handles. `Mul` and `Div` need `Self` on both sides (§8.7). `scale` overflows `Int` on JS for spans whose result fits. Go's `1h2m3s` text was rejected for Rust's one-unit form, which is simpler to specify and to read in logs. Refines D-650.
- **D-652 Waiting for a span of zero or less**: A standard-library function that waits for a `Duration`, such as `fiber::timeout` and `selector::after`, treats a span of zero or less as already elapsed, and a span longer than the runtime's clock can represent as one that never elapses. Why: spans may be negative (D-650) and come from arithmetic such as `deadline - elapsed`, so these functions must not fail on them (D-297). Go's timers behave the same way. Refines D-103, D-650.
- **D-653 Sources of randomness and their state**: `std::random` has the host functions `int_between(low, high) -> Int`, `fraction() -> Float` and `from_host() -> Generator`, and the seeded functions `from_seed(seed: Int) -> Generator`, `next_int_between(generator, low, high) -> (Int, Generator)` and `next_fraction(generator) -> (Float, Generator)` over an opaque `Generator` written in Cheby. The module keeps no state that a program can observe, global or per fiber. Each host call starts from fresh host bits, and a generator's state is its value, so drawing twice from the same value gives the same number. Why: D-226 asks for a seedable generator threaded through calls and for functions that draw from the host. With no hidden state, fibers need no per-fiber generator and no locking, and a seeded program stays reproducible however its fibers are scheduled. A global seeded generator was rejected because fibers draw from it in scheduling order (D-028). A per-fiber generator was rejected because a fiber has no identity a program can see (§9.1). Refines D-226.
- **D-654 Bounds of a random draw include both ends**: `int_between(low, high)` and `next_int_between(generator, low, high)` draw from `low` to `high` with both ends included. When `high` is less than `low`, they draw from `high` to `low`. No pair of bounds fails or panics, except that on JS a result outside ±(2⁵³−1) panics (D-037). The README's conventions say that D-319's rule, "ranges exclude their end", covers ranges given as `start` and `end`, and that bounds given as `low` and `high` include both, as `int::clamp` already does. Why: example 013 calls `int_between(0, max_ms)` to pause from 0 to `max_ms` milliseconds, and calls it with `(0, 0)` in its tests, which an end-excluding range would make empty. Returning `Result` or `Option` would change 013 for a mistake with an obvious meaning. Python's `randint`, Elm's `Random.int` and Rust's `gen_range(a..=b)` include both ends. Refines D-226, D-297, D-319.
- **D-655 The seeded generator is SplitMix64, fixed bit for bit**: A `Generator` is SplitMix64 over a 64-bit state. `from_seed(seed)` uses the seed's two's-complement bits as the state. A step adds `0x9E3779B97F4A7C15` and outputs the mix with the constants `0xBF58476D1CE4E5B9` and `0x94D049BB133111EB` and the shifts 30, 27 and 31. `next_int_between` maps outputs to a range by rejecting those below `(2⁶⁴ − span) % span` and taking `low + x % span`; a span of 2⁶⁴ takes one output as it is. `next_fraction` takes `(x >> 11) / 2⁵³`. All of this is part of the specification, so a seed gives the same numbers on every target and, from 1.0, with every toolchain. Why: D-232 already requires the same seeded results on native and JS, and replaying a simulation or a failing test across versions is the main use of a seed. SplitMix64 needs only `U64` addition, multiplication, xor and right shifts, which are exact on JS too (ADR-0030), and passes BigCrush. Leaving the algorithm unspecified, as Rust's `rand` does, was rejected because seeded output would change silently. PCG64 and Lemire's mapping need 128-bit products, and xoshiro256** needs rotations that `<<` does not give without masking (§5.4.6). Refines D-226, D-232. → [ADR-0056](../adr/0056-seeded-random-generator-is-specified.md)
- **D-656 One private kernel for host randomness**: `std::random` has one kernel, the private `host_bits() -> U64`, bound as `@external(native, "cheby_random_host_bits")` and `@external(js, "./random.mjs", "host_bits")`. It returns 64 bits from the operating system's random source on native and from `crypto.getRandomValues` on JS. `from_host()` is the generator whose state is those bits, and `int_between` and `fraction` are the seeded functions applied to `from_host()`. `std::random` is not among the modules whose kernels run in constants, so a constant that reaches a host function is E0077, while the seeded functions run in constants. The module promises no cryptographic strength. Why: one kernel keeps the mapping to ranges in Cheby, written once for both targets (D-274). D-090 forbids randomness in constants, and E0077 already reports it. Promising cryptographic strength would bind every later implementation, and keys belong in a module of their own. Refines D-226, D-537.
- **D-657 The functions of `std::runtime`**: `std::runtime` has `thread_count() -> Int`, `set_thread_count(count: Int)` and `cpu_count() -> Int`, all kernels. The thread count is always from 1 to 1024. `set_thread_count` clamps its argument to that range (D-298), applies to the whole program, starts new threads at once, and stops a removed thread after its fiber reaches a suspension point. `cpu_count` is the number of cores the process may use, as Rust's `available_parallelism` reports it, and 1 when the system reports nothing. On JS, `thread_count` is 1, `set_thread_count` has no effect and `cpu_count` is what the host reports. Stack sizes are not set in `std::runtime`. Why: D-175 asks for a runtime function to override the thread count, and a pool sized to the machine needs `cpu_count`. Returning the old count, as Go's `GOMAXPROCS(n)` does, mixes a query into a setter. The cap keeps a typo such as `1000000` from starting a million threads. Refines D-175.
- **D-658 `CHEBY_THREADS`**: The environment variable that sets the scheduler's thread count is `CHEBY_THREADS`. The runtime reads it once, before `main` starts, in `cheby run`, `cheby test` and the REPL alike. Unset or empty means `cpu_count()`, capped at 1024. A decimal whole number of at least 1 sets the count, clamped to 1024. Any other value stops the program before `main` runs, with `error: CHEBY_THREADS must be a whole number of at least 1, not "<value>"` on standard error and exit status 1. JS does not read it. It does not affect `-j`, which sizes the compiler's own workers (D-622). Why: D-175 names a variable "like `GOMAXPROCS`" without naming it, and M2's exit criterion needs one. The `CHEBY_RT_` prefix stays for debugging switches such as `CHEBY_RT_STATS` (D-635), and this one is a production setting. A misspelled setting is never ignored silently (D-444), so Go's choice, to ignore it, was rejected. Status 1 is the status of a program that failed without a panic (§10.10). Refines D-175.
- **D-659 Per-spawn stack size**: `fiber::spawn_with_stack(owner, stack_bytes, f)` spawns with a stack of `stack_bytes`, clamped to the range of D-660 (`@std_clamped(stack_bytes)`) and rounded up to the page size. It has no effect on JS. Detached fibers always get the default. Why: D-125 and §10.1 make the stack configurable per spawn, and plan workstream 2.4 implements it. A separate function keeps `spawn` as the examples use it. A detached variant is left out because no use is known, and adding one later breaks nothing. Refines D-125.
- **D-660 Setting stack sizes**: The manifest gains an optional `[runtime]` table with `fiber-stack`, the reservation of every fiber that does not set its own, 8 MiB by default, and `main-stack`, the reservation of the root fiber running `main`, of each test and of each REPL entry, 64 MiB by default in the reference implementation. A value has the form of `memory-limit` (D-536), must be from 1 MiB to 16 GiB, and is rounded up to whole pages. A value outside that range or of the wrong form is E0036, and so is any other key in the table (D-444). Only the table of the package being run or tested applies; in a dependency it is checked and has no effect. On JS it has no effect. `fiber::spawn_with_stack` clamps to the same range (D-298, D-659). Why: D-125 makes the sizes configurable at build time and per spawn but says neither where nor how. A library cannot know the stack its user's program needs, so only the root package decides. The lower bound keeps the entry check's headroom for foreign calls a small part of the stack (D-457, ADR-0032). The upper bound keeps a reservation inside every 64-bit address space with room for other fibers. Refines D-125, D-659. Extends D-444, D-536.
- **D-661 Tier-2 modules on JS before M4**: Every kernel and intrinsic of a tier-2 module declares its JS binding, `@external(js, "./<module>.mjs", "<function>")`, next to the native one, although the `.mjs` files are written in M4 with the JS back end, as for tier 1 (D-450). So every function the tier-2 modules export exists on both targets for the cross-target check (E0067). `std::duration` and the seeded half of `std::random` are written in Cheby and need nothing. Why: without the binding, every package that keeps the default `targets` and imports a tier-2 module gets E0067 (D-480, D-512), and editing std again in M4 is what D-450 avoids. Extends D-450.
- **D-662 Checking the code of the stdlib spec**: `cargo xtask cross-check` also collects every ` ```cheby ` block in `docs/stdlib/**/*.md` and parses it as module items with the compiler's parser and with tree-sitter, and both must accept it, as for the examples. Validation errors, such as a signature without a body (E0046), are not consulted (D-424). A spec test is written so that it checks unchanged, apart from module prefixes, in the std module where D-630 places it, so it binds no name that module declares: `std::fiber`'s tests bind a scope as `owner`, never `scope` (D-051). Why: README line 74 requires the blocks to parse and nothing checks it, and the tier-2 drafts add more than 60 blocks. A test that only compiles outside std would fail the std self-test once its module ships, as the first tier-2 drafts did with E0044. Type-checking the blocks in `cross-check` was rejected for now: the tests run in `std/` once a module ships (D-542, D-630), and `cheby doc` compares signatures from M5 (D-220). Extends D-272, D-615, D-630.
- **D-663 Example 022 keeps its `todo`**: Example 022 keeps `money::apply_interest` and its `todo`, which shows the W0009 warning on purpose (D-068). Its `expected.stderr` holds the warning that `cheby run` prints, blessed like any golden (D-292). The clean check of examples in `cargo xtask check-examples` and in the driver's `examples_check_cleanly` allows exactly one W0009 at `src/money.cheby` in 022, and nothing else. Why: 022 has the only `todo` in the examples, and its doc comment explains the warning, so removing it loses the lesson. Without the golden, its run would fail the stderr comparison as soon as it runs in M2. The clean rule that `xtask/src/examples.rs` attributes to D-477 is not in D-477's text, so this entry writes it down: every example checks with no diagnostic, warnings included, except this one. Refines D-068, D-477.
- **D-664 The Cheby collections ship in M2**: Workstream 2.9 implements and ships `std::sorted_map`, `std::sorted_set`, `std::sorted_multimap`, `std::ordered_map`, `std::ordered_set`, `std::ordered_multimap` and `std::multimap`, written in Cheby as tier 1 specifies them (D-336, D-338). They come early in M2, because they need nothing from the runtime work. Their benchmarks run once with M1's naive reference counting, as part of the baseline, and again with Perceus reuse, against a Rust tree, to settle OQ-P13 at M2 exit. Shipping them is an M2 exit criterion, after which importing them is no longer E0038 (D-480). Why: OQ-P13 can only be settled by measuring the Cheby collections, which do not exist yet, and nothing scheduled them. Staging them unshipped (D-486) was rejected, because a benchmark of the real modules needs them imported. Refines D-336, D-480.
- **D-665 Scope bodies and timeouts run on the calling fiber**: `scope` calls its body, and `timeout` calls its function, as ordinary calls on the calling fiber, with that fiber's stack. A timeout's scope has no `Scope` value, so its only member is the function. Values captured or returned by these functions are not marked shared. Why: §10.3 already cancels "the scope's body" with the scope, which only makes sense for frames of the caller. Running `timeout`'s function on a child fiber would give a deep recursion inside `timeout` in `main` 8 MiB instead of 64 MiB, cost a fiber and a marking traversal per call, and make unwinding stop at a fiber boundary for timeouts but mid-stack for scopes. A child fiber per `timeout`, as a Go goroutine would be, was rejected. Refines D-040, D-103. → [ADR-0057](../adr/0057-scope-bodies-run-on-the-calling-fiber.md)
- **D-666 Scope lifecycle, spawning and caught panics**: A scope is open from the call of its body until the body has finished and every fiber spawned into it has ended. Any fiber holding the `Scope` may spawn into it meanwhile, also while the scope waits, and those fibers are waited for too. Spawning into a scope that has failed or been cancelled creates a fiber that is cancelled before it starts, so its function is never called. Spawning into a scope that has ended panics with `spawn into a scope that has ended`. A scope records the first panic when it starts, before the panicking member unwinds, and cancels the other members at that moment; later panics are not recorded. A scope frame is never removed, by return or by unwinding, while any of its fibers runs. A panic that a scope or a timeout catches is not printed. Why: §10.3 says only that spawning after the end panics. It leaves open spawning during the wait, which worker pools and crawlers need, and spawning into a failing scope. Recording at the start makes "first" independent of how long unwinding takes. Not printing caught panics is what 019's empty-stderr golden requires. The panic is a rule of scopes, not a bad argument (D-297): running the function detached would break structured concurrency, and dropping it silently would lose work. Refines D-040, D-176, D-178.
- **D-667 Cancellation targets and nested scopes**: Every cancellation targets an open scope: a failing scope targets itself, and an expired timeout its own scope. Cancelling a scope cancels its fibers and its body. A cancelled body unwinds the calling fiber down to the target's `scope` or `timeout` call, which returns `Err`; a cancelled fiber unwinds completely. Scopes that a cancelled fiber or body has open are cancelled too, with the same target, and the unwinding passes through their frames without returning from them, waiting at each until its fibers have ended. A fiber with several pending cancellations unwinds to the outermost target, and one that arrives during an unwinding for an inner target extends it. Detached fibers are never reached. Why: §10.7 says cancellation cannot be caught, yet scopes stop the unwinding of their own failures, and nothing said what an inner scope does when an outer scope or timeout cancels it. Returning `Err` from the inner scope would let code run after a cancellation, contradicting D-102. Refines D-040, D-102, D-103.
- **D-668 `join` from outside the fiber's scope**: `join` may be called by any fiber, any number of times, and returns the same value each time. If the joined fiber panicked or was cancelled, a joiner that is being cancelled unwinds, which is always the case for a member of that fiber's scope, and any other joiner panics with `joined fiber failed`. §10.3's `join` paragraph states this as a language rule. Why: D-177's "the joiner is cancelled" holds only inside the failing scope, but a `Fiber` can travel over channels or outlive its scope, and `join` returns `T`, so there is no value to return. Waiting forever was rejected because it hides the failure. The panic is a rule of the language, like spawning into an ended scope, so it meets D-297, which allows std panics only where a language rule makes them. Refines D-177, D-297.
- **D-669 Detached fibers**: `spawn_detached<T>(f: fn() -> T)` returns `Nil` and releases `f`'s result when it returns, so `f` may return `Never`. No cancellation reaches a detached fiber, including a timeout or scope around the code that spawned it. Its panic is printed to standard error as a panic report (§11.6), after standard output is flushed, with the two lines written together. Why: 019 discards the result, and returning a `Fiber` would add joins of fibers outside every scope, whose failure has no scope to cancel. §10.4's "reported to standard error" named no format. Refines D-040, D-073.
- **D-670 When the fiber API releases values**: The fiber that runs a spawned function releases it no later than when the call returns or unwinds. `scope` releases its body and `timeout` its function when the call returns, before waiting or returning. A fiber's result is kept only while a `Fiber` value for it exists, and otherwise is released when the function returns. A cancelled `send` has not enqueued its value, which unwinding releases, and a cancelled `receive`, `select` or `join` takes nothing. When the last `Receiver` of a channel is dropped, the values in its buffer are released at once, on that fiber. `select` releases its reference to the selector, and so its receivers, before it calls the chosen mapping function. Work the runtime cannot interrupt (file IO, standard input and `@blocking` calls) finishes before a cancellation is delivered, and its result is released, so `timeout` waits for it. Why: examples depend on these release points (005's and 018's shutdowns, and 009's and 013's tests that check a channel is closed right after a timeout), and §9.2's last-use guarantee covers bindings, not the runtime's own references. Refines D-054, D-102, D-103, D-205.
- **D-671 Timeout details**: `timeout` returns `Ok` if the function returns before a cancellation reaches it, even after the limit, and `Err(Panicked(p))` if it panics first. Otherwise it returns `Err(TimedOut)` once everything inside has unwound, never earlier than the limit after the call, and a panic during that unwinding does not change the result. A limit of zero or less returns `Err(TimedOut)` without calling the function, on every target. Fibers the function spawns into scopes from outside it, and detached fibers, are not cancelled. Why: §10.8 left open the race between completion and expiry, zero limits, and the meaning of "every fiber it spawned". Deciding by the delivery of the cancellation makes `TimedOut` mean that the function did not finish. A zero limit that still ran the function up to its first yield check would differ between native and JS. Refines D-103, D-181.
- **D-672 Channel capacity, order and drop functions**: A negative capacity counts as 0, as a clamped count (`@std_clamped(capacity)`, so W0013 warns about a negative literal). A channel delivers values in the order they were enqueued or handed off; which of several waiting receivers gets a value is unspecified. The drop functions of `Sender` and `Receiver` close the channel and wake its waiters, and never block or suspend. §10.5's sentence "a negative capacity panics" is replaced. Why: that sentence has no decision behind it and contradicts D-297 and D-298, under which a count outside its range is clamped. Drop functions run during unwinding and on any fiber (D-171), so they must not wait. Refines D-054, D-298.
- **D-673 Selector semantics**: A selector is an immutable value that can be selected any number of times. A timer arm's clock starts when `select` starts waiting, anew in each call, and a duration of zero or less is ready at once. Any number of timer arms is allowed. A selector without arms is never ready, so `select` waits until the fiber is cancelled. When several arms are ready, `select` chooses one uniformly at random. The chosen mapping function is called exactly once, on the calling fiber, after the value is taken, and a panic in it is an ordinary panic there. `select` takes one parameter, so it pipes bare. Why: §10.6 left these open, and "should not systematically favor any arm" needs a definition that can be tested; Go uses the same uniform choice. Starting the clock at `after` would make building a selector read the clock and give a reused selector a deadline that has already passed; a deadline across several selects is a `timeout` around them. Panicking on an empty selector was rejected by D-297, and waiting until cancelled matches Go's `select {}`. Refines D-041, D-103, D-180, D-206.
- **D-674 Fiber results are shared**: Besides values sent on a channel and captured by a spawned function, a fiber's result becomes shared, because another fiber reads it through `join`. The body of `scope` and the function of `timeout` run on the calling fiber, so their captures and results are not marked. A `Panic` is built on the fiber that reads it, from the runtime's record, so it never crosses fibers. Why: D-072 lists the ways a value reaches another fiber, and `join` is one more. Refines D-072.
- **D-675 How the fiber API calls into Cheby**: `scope`'s body, `timeout`'s function and `select`'s mapping function are ordinary Cheby calls on the calling fiber, and a spawned function is the base of its fiber's stack. A panic or cancellation in them unwinds through the `std` frames between them and the caller as §11.3 and D-667 describe, stopping at the `scope` or `timeout` call where the rules say so. The functions of `std::fiber` and `std::selector` that take functions are written in Cheby over private runtime operations, `@std_context` kernels (D-692) and one compiler intrinsic (D-693), none of which calls a closure. Why: a closure called from a Rust kernel frame would put a frame on the fiber's stack that the unwinder cannot cross and that pins the fiber to its thread. D-484's rule that only `map::upsert` calls a closure from a kernel stays as it is.
- **D-676 Fiber context switch**: Fibers switch with our own `naked_asm!` switch per platform: x86-64 SysV, including Mach-O, and AAPCS64 on Linux and Apple, with Win64 and Windows aarch64 in M6. It is called only from Rust, saves the C callee-saved set, and is used asymmetrically between a fiber and its worker's stack, carrying a post-switch action. corosensei behind the same API is the fallback if an assembly bug blocks the slice. Why: corosensei lacks Windows aarch64, assumes a guard page per stack (D-124 rejects them), aborts by forcing Rust unwinds through dropped coroutines, is `!Send` and has no TSan hooks. wasmtime-fiber owns its stacks, and Cranelift's `stack_switch` is x64-only. Settles OQ-P7. → [ADR-0058](../adr/0058-own-fiber-context-switch.md)
- **D-677 Stack slots and regions**: Stacks come in power-of-two classes from 512 KiB to 16 GiB, carved from shared `MAP_NORESERVE` regions with one guard page per region. The fiber control block sits at the top of its slot. Regions are never unmapped while the process runs. Free slots are reused LIFO, and pages below a depth watermark are released on reuse (`MADV_DONTNEED` on Linux, a `MAP_FIXED` remap on macOS). User-requested sizes are clamped to 1 MiB to 16 GiB, the range D-660 gives D-659. Strict overcommit (`vm.overcommit_memory=2`, where Linux ignores `MAP_NORESERVE`) is not supported: a region that cannot be mapped makes `spawn` panic with `out of memory`. Why: this keeps D-124's single compare and small map count and honors ADR-0032's release. The FCB needs no allocation, and a stale FCB pointer always points to mapped memory. Committing slots one by one with `mprotect` was rejected because it splits regions into many mappings and costs a system call per new slot. Refines D-124, D-125.
- **D-678 Debug guard pages**: Builds with debug assertions protect the lowest page of every slot, unless `CHEBY_RT_STACK_GUARD=0`. Release builds keep D-124's no-guard rule. Why: it turns the one silent hazard of guard-free stacks, Rust or foreign code overflowing below the last entry check, into a crash in tests. Refines D-124.
- **D-679 Large-frame entry guard**: A function whose frame exceeds 64 KiB is compiled as a small outer function, whose entry check accounts for the frame, and an unchecked inner body that the outer one tail-calls. Why: the entry check runs after the prologue has allocated the frame, so a frame above about 192 KiB would call the slow path below its slot. Cranelift's own stack-limit trap would need signal handling. Refines D-124, D-453.
- **D-680 IO poller**: Each runtime has a Go-style integrated poller on `mio` 1.x. One idle worker blocks in it with the next timer as its timeout, and the others park. M2 registers the waker and timers; io_uring is not used in v1. Why: mio is the proven cross-platform readiness layer, Tokio's backend, with Windows solved. io_uring is Linux-only, often disabled by seccomp, and completion-based, so a cancelled fiber would wait before unwinding could free its buffer. A condvar-only M2 poller would be rewritten in M3. Settles OQ-P8. Refines D-042. → [ADR-0059](../adr/0059-integrated-io-poller-on-mio.md)
- **D-681 Sockets move to M3**: Plan workstream 2.6's poller covers timers and the waker in M2. Socket registration arrives with `std::net` in M3, on the same seam. `@blocking` stays in M2. Why: no M2 module or example uses sockets, and `std::net` is tier 3. The user approved moving it. A private socket registration API in M2, unit-tested over a Rust `TcpListener`, was rejected, because nothing in M2 would use it. Refines plan workstream 2.6.
- **D-682 Timers**: Each runtime keeps one timer heap under a mutex, ordered by deadline and then by a sequence number. Cancellation is lazy, through the waiter's state word, and the clock is monotonic. A `select` arms one timer, for its earliest timer arm. Std passes spans to the runtime as `(seconds, nanoseconds)`, which the runtime saturates, as D-652 requires. Why: correct and simple, with a deterministic order for equal deadlines. Per-worker heaps wait for measurements (D-731). Refines D-103.
- **D-683 Blocking pool and `@blocking`**: One process-global pool of up to 512 threads, started on demand with a 10 s idle exit, runs blocking work. Jobs return Rust data, and their completion state lives on the heap, never on the fiber's stack. Standard input, the four file kernels and `@blocking` externals use it; `@blocking` is threaded through THIR. A cancellation arriving during a job waits for it. Without a runtime, a job runs inline on the calling thread. Why: D-191 keeps pool threads out of Cheby code, and exact accounting domains need Cheby values built on fibers. A job may complete after its fiber was stopped, so it must not write into the fiber's slot. Refines D-042.
- **D-684 Standard streams under fibers**: Each write call writes its whole text under the stream lock, which is never held across a switch. D-574's state is kept under the stdout lock. A stalled stdout pipe stalls every fiber that prints, and can stop every worker; this is accepted in M2. Why: one call is one write, so the output of concurrent fibers never interleaves inside a call. Handing writes that would block to the pool waits for measurements with M3's servers (D-731). Refines D-346, D-574.
- **D-685 The fiber control block**: `ExecCtx` is the fiber control block (FCB). Its generated-code view has fixed offsets: 0 for the stack limit, 8 for the mode and 12 for the unwind flag, which stays reserved whichever unwinding backend is chosen (D-696). Interrupt reasons and every per-entry thread-local of M1 move into its Rust part. Isolated entries keep a heap-allocated FCB. Why: thread-local state is wrong once fibers migrate between threads, and D-453 already anticipates the fiber's context. Refines D-453, D-526, D-564.
- **D-686 Thread-local discipline**: `tls.rs` is the only module of `cheby_runtime` that declares thread-locals, the allocator's caches included. All access goes through `#[inline(never)]` accessors, and a source test checks the whole crate. Why: LLVM may reuse a thread-local's address across an opaque switch, including inside inlined allocator code, which after a migration is another thread's cache.
- **D-687 Migration and pinning**: Fibers migrate freely between workers, except while a kernel callback is on their stack, when they are pinned to their worker. A fiber suspends only through `park`, which releases its lock after the switch. A migration stress mode runs under TSan. Why: kernel frames under a callback are tier-1 code written for one thread, and callbacks are short. Every suspending runtime function follows the switch rules, so M:N balancing still works for 005, 013 and 022. Refines D-526.
- **D-688 Interrupt reasons and preemption**: The FCB holds a reason word, set and taken with a sequentially consistent protocol, with the reasons yield, cancel, deadline and safepoint. Cancellation is level-triggered from the fiber's cancellation target. A monitor thread per runtime requests a yield from a fiber that has run 10 ms. Why: M1's request carries no reason, and release-only orderings can lose an interrupt. Refines D-028, D-195, D-453.
- **D-689 Scheduler structure and thread count**: Each worker has a runnext slot, a Chase-Lev deque and a pinned queue; the runtime adds a global injector, and idle workers steal half. Workers park with Tokio's idle protocol. `Runtime::new` reads `CHEBY_THREADS` (D-658), and `set_thread_count` (D-657) starts workers or retires them at their next scheduling point. Why: Go's and Tokio's proven structures. Runnext keeps capacity-0 ping-pong on one worker, and the pinned queue serves D-687. Refines D-012, D-175.
- **D-690 One runtime per host run**: Each `cheby run`, test run and REPL session has its own `Runtime`. The stack pool, the blocking pool, the layout table and D-642's reserve are process-global. Program arguments move into the runtime. Isolated entries keep dedicated threads. Why: in-process hosts, such as the driver's parallel unit tests, `test-std` and REPL sessions, run concurrently and each needs its own shutdown. Refines D-457.
- **D-691 Accounting domains**: Each root, test, REPL session and constant evaluation has its own accounting domain, which spawned fibers inherit. Counters are sharded per worker and summed when read. Why: exact and isolated leak counts, and a D-617 limit that detached fibers cannot disturb, without a shared cache line on every allocation. Refines D-564, D-617.
- **D-692 `@std_context` kernels**: `@std_context` is a std-only attribute on an `@external` function. Its kernel gets the running fiber's `ctx` as its first argument and may suspend, raise, use fiber state or start fibers. The tier-2 runtime kernels and the suspending io kernels carry it. Without a runtime (Windows before M6, the REPL until it moves onto the runtime, isolated entries), an operation that completes without waiting works, a blocking-pool job runs inline, and one that would start a fiber, wait or arm a timer ends the program with a fatal runtime error naming the operation. This settles how D-675's private operations are bound. Why: suspending code gets an explicit fiber and no thread-local lookups, and every host has a defined behavior. A fatal error is used instead of a panic because `scope` would catch a panic and mislead. D-484 and D-661 still apply. Refines D-453, D-482.
- **D-693 Std intrinsics and the scope sequence**: A std external whose native symbol is in a fixed table of `cheby_rt_` intrinsics gets a compiler-generated body instead of a foreign call. M2 has one intrinsic, `scope_run`. It opens a scope, calls the body with an unwind edge and closes the scope, with no Cheby call in between, and returns a static `Err(Nil)` on failure. The cause stays in the fiber, and all catch policy is in the runtime's scope close. `scope_open` may raise only out of memory, before anything is open. This implements D-665 and D-666. Why: scope bodies run under JIT frames only, no point between open and close can deliver a cancellation, catching allocates nothing, and no new attribute is needed. A kernel calling the body, an open-catch-close sequence in Cheby, and a general `catch` intrinsic, which would also catch cancellations, were rejected. Refines D-102, D-455, D-520. → [ADR-0060](../adr/0060-std-intrinsics-and-the-fused-scope-sequence.md)
- **D-694 The base frame of a spawned fiber**: The runtime's `fiber_main` calls the spawned function through `cb$tramp$0`, drops the function, stores or drops its result, and reports or records a panic. It then drops every Cheby reference the FCB holds, and only after that leaves its scope's members or the detached set, which is its last touch of shared state. Why: a runtime entering Cheby at a fiber's base is not a kernel calling a closure, so D-484 stays. The order makes every release happen before the scope owner wakes and before a leak check reads the domain. Refines D-526.
- **D-695 Selectors**: A `Selector` is a persistent list of runtime nodes, built by private kernels in O(1). `select` calls a kernel that starts the timer clocks, picks uniformly at random among ready arms, moves the chosen value into the fiber and returns the arm's apply closure, which Cheby calls after the selector is released. This implements D-673. Why: no type-erasing cast is needed, closure calls stay out of kernels, and only a kernel can make a value of an external type (D-590). Refines D-041, D-180, D-206.
- **D-696 Two unwinding backends are prototyped and measured**: Two backends for D-077's unwinding are built on the same MIR unwind pass (D-698), with the same catch points (D-699) and Rust guards (D-697), and measured as D-702 says. The first is return-flag propagation: Cheby frames unwind by running a cleanup pad and returning 0, and callers test the result of a Cheby call, or the flag at `ctx+12` after a C call. The second is `try_call` with catch-all handlers, generated exit stubs for C callees, a frame-pointer unwinder and a table registry. The user picks one at the slice checkpoint, from the measurements. D-077 and ADR-0026 stay as they are until then. Why: the user chose to measure both first, because each cost is known only from data. A `try_call` to a `Tail` callee clobbers every register, so every value live across a call with cleanup is spilled. C callees need an exit stub on every allocation, the tables need a registry that M3 must serialize, `WindowsFastcall` cannot be a `try_call` callee, and Rust frames need a rethrow protocol. The flag needs none of that, but costs one compare and branch after every call that may unwind and departs from D-077's mechanism.
- **D-697 Rust frames during unwinding**: A Cheby unwinding inside Rust is a Rust unwinding with a zero-sized marker. Every kernel and helper that may unwind runs in a guard at its C-ABI entry point, which catches the marker and hands the unwinding back to the Cheby frames by the means of the backend chosen by the unwinding measurement (D-696): with the return flag, it sets the flag and returns a sentinel. `callback::call` rethrows, and `map::upsert` gets a drop guard. Other Rust panics stay fatal, and the runtime requires `panic = "unwind"`. Why: discarding Rust frames would leak worklists, skew accounting and could leave half-updated maps. Refines D-342, D-526.
- **D-698 MIR unwind edges and shared pads**: A MIR pass after reference counting and Perceus adds per-statement unwind edges to chained cleanup pads, memoized by owned set, an entry pad and a `Resume` terminator. Runtime helpers consume owned arguments even when they raise. The validator checks pad sets against ownership, edges only on operations that can unwind, and reuse tokens consumed once. Why: D-262's shared cleanup, on MIR, independent of the backend. Refines D-262, D-273.
- **D-699 Catch points and the unwind record**: Unwinding stops at entry thunks, trampolines, a fiber's base and the scope intrinsic. The cause lives in the fiber. A panic is recorded in its responsible scope when it starts, and a cancellation unwinds to its outermost target. This implements D-666 and D-667. Why: a cause kept in the fiber needs no allocation while unwinding, recording at the start gives D-666's "first panic", and the outermost target is D-667's rule. Refines D-176, D-178, D-457, D-526.
- **D-700 Unwinding after a stack overflow**: An overflow unwinds within the 256 KiB headroom, whichever backend the unwinding measurement chooses (D-696), and tests bound the raise path to 32 KiB. Why: the report and the `Panic` are built at the catch point, high on the stack, so only the raise path and the cleanup pads run at depth. Unwinding by returning needs no extra stack, a frame-pointer unwinder needs a small fixed amount, and only kernel frames below a callback use Rust's unwinder. Refines D-124, D-457.
- **D-701 Panic reports at the catch point**: A panic's report is printed where its unwinding ends: by root and REPL entries and by detached fibers, after flushing standard output. Tests and constants capture it, and scopes and timeouts stay silent. Why: 019 catches panics with an empty-stderr golden, and today's report at the panic's start cannot know whether a scope will catch it. Refines D-449, D-457, D-595.
- **D-702 Measuring the unwinding backends**: Both backends of D-696 are prototyped on the same MIR pass (D-698). The first is return-flag propagation. The second is `try_call` with catch-all handlers, exit stubs, a frame-pointer unwinder and a table registry. Each is measured against the naive baseline without unwinding: `bench-compile` throughput on the 100k and 1M shapes against the last commit before the unwind pass, and geometric-mean run time on `bench/runtime`'s RC group against the same build with a hidden measurement switch that ignores the unwind edges, which release builds honor because `bench-run` measures a release `cheby`. The numbers go to the user at the slice checkpoint, and the user picks the backend. A cost of at most 3% of throughput and at most 3% of run time is the reference against which the numbers are read. Why: D-644 left 4% to 7% above D-247's floor for all of M2, and measuring both backends against one baseline lets the choice rest on data. Refines D-644.
- **D-703 Handle kinds and finalizers**: `Sender`, `Receiver`, `Fiber`, `Scope` and `Selector` values are runtime kinds 40 to 44. Each has a `handle` flag, which gives D-233's behavior through the external-value path, and a finalizer run from the free worklist that never blocks, suspends, raises or runs Cheby code. The same finalizer slot serves user external drop functions in M3. Why: one shared external kind cannot tell handle types apart, and kinds without equality would hit a fatal error today. Refines D-101, D-171, D-590.
- **D-704 Channels**: A channel is Go's `hchan`: a per-channel lock, a buffer, and FIFO queues of intrusive nodes on the waiting fibers' stacks, claimed by CAS. Selector nodes share a done word. Values are marked shared outside the lock, and several channels are locked in address order. Why: production-proven, and Cheby stacks do not move (D-124). Refines D-054, D-206.
- **D-705 Shared marking**: An iterative `mark_shared` runs on values sent on channels, spawned functions, fiber results and REPL bindings. Uniqueness stays a count of 1, and sharing stays sticky in M2, so ADR-0024 is unchanged. Why: a positive count stays confined to one fiber, so inline reference counting needs no atomics, and these are the only points where a value reaches another fiber. Making shared values local again waits for measurements (D-731). Refines D-072.
- **D-706 Scope tree and cancellation**: Scopes link their parent, owner, members and nested scopes. First causes are recorded at a panic's start. Cancellation is a parent-first locked walk that sets the outermost target. A fiber's cancellation target and wait node are read and written only under the fiber's own leaf lock, so a scope's close and an outer cancellation are ordered and none is lost. Close waits without interruption, then returns or raises again. This implements D-666, D-667 and D-671. Why: level-triggered targets cannot be lost only if clearing a target is ordered with every writer. Refines D-040, D-102.
- **D-707 Fiber results and `join`**: A fiber's result lives in a join cell shared with its `Fiber` handles, is marked shared, and is released with the last handle. `join` follows D-668's failure rules. Why: a cell that outlives the fiber lets any holder join any number of times, and releasing with the last handle keeps D-670's release point. Refines D-177.
- **D-708 Stopping fibers and the lifetime of code**: A JIT's code memory belongs to a shared keep-alive token and is freed by its last holder. Every runtime holds the tokens of the JITs it runs and releases them only after its fibers have stopped. Fibers are stopped by teardown, cancelling and unwinding them, everywhere except right before the process exits, where release builds freeze them without unwinding (D-073) and never reuse their slots. Why: 019 would crash at exit if code were freed under a detached fiber. The REPL compiles into one JIT with `&mut` while a session-long runtime runs fibers, so a borrow cannot express the lifetime. A frozen fiber leaves wait nodes and timers pointing into its stack, which is safe only if nothing runs afterwards. Refines D-073, D-457.
- **D-709 Termination as an outcome**: D-306's "ends at once" freezes the failing fiber, stops the world and returns status 1 to the host. The runtime never calls `process::exit`. Why: libc `exit` would race running workers and kill in-process hosts. Refines D-306.
- **D-710 Leak checks with fibers**: The debug leak check also runs after a panic, and a leak turns 101 into 102. Debug builds tear detached fibers down before the check, without user external drop functions. The check is skipped and counted when a fiber is stuck on the blocking pool. §10.10 notes that an implementation may tear detached fibers down at exit instead of killing them, which no program can observe. Why: exact checks for 019 and the panicking examples, and D-445's bless guard keeps refusing leak lines. Refines D-073, D-458.
- **D-711 Leaked runtime-object report**: After D-458's line, debug builds add one line naming the leaked runtime kinds. No allowance list is added until an example needs one. Why: it names the handles behind a leak, which D-029 asks implementations to document, at no cost when nothing leaks. Refines D-029, D-458.
- **D-712 REPL on the runtime**: A REPL session has one JIT and one runtime for its whole life. Entries unwind. Slots are written with release stores, replaced values are kept, and session values are marked shared. At `:reload` and at session end, the session's detached fibers are torn down, in every build, before any session value is dropped, and `:reload` keeps the JIT and the runtime. Why: reloaded modules are new versions in the same session (D-625), and a frozen fiber's wait node in a channel that a session value still reaches would be a use-after-free. Supersedes D-283. Refines D-194, D-457, D-613.
- **D-713 Constant evaluation**: Constant evaluation stays on D-457's dedicated thread, with unwinding and a fresh domain per evaluation. Why: constants cannot reach fiber operations (E0077, D-537). Running them inline on a stack slot is a measured follow-up (D-731). Refines D-457, D-617.
- **D-714 Windows until M6**: The runtime builds on Windows with stubs. Entries use the thread backend, with unwinding and D-692's rule for fibers without a runtime, and the fatal error adds a note that fibers on Windows arrive in M6. D-641 extends to runtime tests that switch stacks or run a scheduler. Why: D-545 leaves Windows fibers to M6, and programs that start no fiber keep working. Refines D-545, D-641.
- **D-715 Borrowing**: Parameters may be borrowed only in module-private functions that are never used as values, not in the REPL and not external wrappers, only for handle-free parameter types, and only where a tail-call fixed point allows. The borrow mask is part of the ABI signature and the slot key. Why: borrowing removes dup and drop pairs at calls, but the slot convention, tail calls, handle close timing (D-205) and in-place kernels (D-525) limit where it is sound. Refines D-072, D-250, D-456.
- **D-716 Reuse, fusion and specialization**: `drop_reuse` yields tokens that are consumed once on every path, pads included, and never for shared objects. Fusion and constructor-specialized drops run with a check that no drop of a possibly-handle-holding value moves later. Why: D-005's reuse, checked by the validator, without breaking ADR-0024 or D-205's close timing. Refines D-005, D-262.
- **D-717 Reuse report**: `--reuse-report` on `cheby run` and `cheby test` prints one sorted line per reuse candidate to standard error, with static lines for kernel preconditions, and UI goldens pin it. Why: D-170 asks for a report but names neither flag nor format, and sorted lines are testable. Refines D-170.
- **D-718 Differential oracle, fuzz target and benchmarks**: Naive reference counting stays selectable, with a conformance variant and a CI job under it. A program generator and a naive-versus-Perceus fuzz target are added. `bench/runtime` records the M1 baseline before any Perceus or unwinding change, and `bench-compile` gains size and RC columns. Why: workstream 2.1 asks for differential checks against M1's naive reference counting, and benchmarks need a baseline recorded before anything changes. Refines D-273.
- **D-719 The reference-evaluator fuzzer moves to M3**: M2 delivers the program generator and the naive-versus-Perceus target. The comparison with a reference evaluator, which `testing.md` §8 asks for from M2, moves to M3. Why: no M2 risk needs it beyond 2.1's differential check, and it costs about 2,000 lines. The user approved moving it. Building an evaluator for the generator's subset in M2 was rejected for that cost. Refines `testing.md` §8.
- **D-720 Compile-time debts**: MIR boxes tuple fields after the single allocation, and lowering splits very large functions into chained private continuations. Why: these are the open items of D-643's session; a size budget bounds regalloc2's superlinear cost and keeps frames small for D-679. Refines D-643, D-644.
- **D-721 Sanitizers, Miri and nightly fuzzing**: A scheduled workflow on a dated nightly runs TSan and ASan on Linux x86-64 with fiber annotations, a Miri subset and bounded fuzz runs. JIT code is not instrumented, and the M2 exit report says so. Why: sanitizers need a nightly toolchain, which D-277's stable pin forbids for every other job, and a scheduled run never blocks a pull request. Refines D-277 for that workflow only.
- **D-722 Thread variants**: The conformance harness runs every example's run and `::test` trials with `CHEBY_THREADS` unset, 1 and 2, with the same goldens. The variants land before the scheduler. The harness bounds its own parallelism while variants run, and `CHEBY_HARNESS_SCALE` multiplies its timeouts. Why: it is an M2 exit criterion, and landing the variants first tests every later package at several thread counts from its first commit.
- **D-723 Parallel `cheby test`**: Tests are root fibers, at most the thread count at once, with output captured and replayed in source order, per-test domains, and detached fibers torn down when their test ends, in every build. §13.6 and §10.4 say that a test's detached fibers end with the test. Why: §13.6 and the plan ask for parallel tests, and replay keeps M1's output and the UI goldens unchanged. A test is a root like `main` (D-040, D-073), and freezing its fibers would leave wait nodes and timers linked while other tests run. Completion-order output was rejected as nondeterministic. Refines D-541, D-598. → [ADR-0062](../adr/0062-parallel-tests-with-ordered-output.md)
- **D-724 A global deadlock is fatal in every build**: Every build detects a global deadlock: no fiber running or runnable, no timer, blocking-pool job or IO registration pending, and the root not finished. `cheby run` then writes `fatal error: all fibers are blocked` to standard error, followed by one line per blocked fiber that names the operation and its location, such as `  blocked in channel::receive at src/main.cheby:12:5`, in a deterministic order, and exits with status 103. A deadlock is not a panic: it cannot be caught, and nothing unwinds before the exit. `cheby test` fails the test with that report, then cancels and unwinds the test's fibers, as D-708 does when a test ends, and continues with the other tests. The REPL reports it, cancels the entry's fibers and continues. §10.4 gains a normative "Deadlock" paragraph, the spec's list of exit statuses gains 103, and §13 states the behavior of `cheby test` and the REPL. Why: the user chose it. A split between debug and release builds means that a program that fails in testing hangs in production. A catchable panic in the root fiber would hide a whole-program failure. Exit status 101 stays for panics, so that scripts can tell the two apart. Detection in debug builds only with a hang in release builds, a panic in the root fiber, and exit status 101 were rejected. Extends D-458.
- **D-725 Timing-sensitive trials**: Examples marked `timing = true` in `test.toml` run their variant trials serially after the parallel batch, and sanitizer runs are serial with scaled timeouts. Why: 009, 013 and 019 wait 250 ms to 1 s and flake under load, and an example is never changed to make a timing test pass.
- **D-726 REPL transcript milestones**: A REPL transcript header accepts `// @milestone: M<n>`, with M1 as the default. Why: D-638's header has no milestone key, so an M2 transcript would fail until M2 ends (D-288). Extends D-638.
- **D-727 Scope of `check-determinism`**: `check-determinism` stays a check of build outputs. Run-time determinism across thread counts is checked by the thread variants. Why: D-551 and D-620 define it as a build check, and D-722's variants already compare run-time output across thread counts. Refines D-620.
- **D-728 Pending stdlib spec tests**: A list of `(module, test, milestone)` lets the spec-test guard accept a test of a shipped module as absent while that milestone is in progress. Past it, any remaining entry fails the guard, and an entry whose test is present fails too. Why: a module must ship before all its behavior exists, and D-486's staging makes a module unimportable. Extends D-288, D-479, D-542.
- **D-729 Unshipped-module probes**: Tests and docs that use `std::fiber` as the example of an unshipped module switch to `std::http` in the package that ships `std::fiber`. Why: `std::fiber` ships in M2, and `std::http` stays unshipped until tier 3. Refines D-480.
- **D-730 Runtime dependencies**: `cheby_runtime` adds `mio` 1.x, `parking_lot` 0.12, `crossbeam-deque` 0.8 and `crossbeam-utils` 0.8, and `loom` as a dev-dependency for model tests of the waiter CAS, the idle counters and the close-versus-outer-cancel race. No `wasmtime-internal-*` crate is used. Why: these are the components of Tokio and rayon, two of them already in `Cargo.lock`, and wasmtime's internal crates have no stable API.
- **D-731 Optimizations that wait for measurements**: These wait for benchmark data, each with its own entry: a count of −1 becoming local again on a uniqueness test, inline constant evaluation on stack slots, direct fiber-to-fiber handoff, per-worker timer heaps, and handing stdout writes that would block to the blocking pool. Why: each trades complexity for speed that only benchmarks can show.
- **D-732 Locations of std-raised panics**: `spawn into a scope that has ended` and `joined fiber failed` report `<unknown location>`, as other kernel panics do (§11.6). Why: reporting the caller's location would need caller-location passing, which no M2 need justifies.

Open:

- ~~Unwinding backend, return flag or `try_call` (D-077, ADR-0026): both are prototyped and measured per D-702, and the user decides at the slice checkpoint. If the return flag wins, D-453 is refined so that an unboxed-result entry point tests the flag at `ctx+12` after the call instead of its result, which can legitimately be 0; the uniform-word entry keeps the result test. The ADR for the chosen backend is written then. Until then no ADR-0061 exists: ADR-0062 (D-723) keeps its number, and 0061 stays unused for now.~~ → D-733 (ADR-0061), D-734.
- OQ-P13 is settled at M2 exit from the collection benchmarks (D-664).
- Deadlock report locations (D-724, D-732): D-724's report names the location of each blocked fiber's call, but D-732 gives std operations no caller location in M2. The mechanism, such as call-site locations passed to suspending `@std_context` kernels or a map from return addresses to locations walked on the blocked fiber, needs the user's decision before H1. The spec marks it OQ-10-11, and the location in §10.4's example is illustrative until a run produces it.
- Deadlocks in parallel tests (D-723, D-724): D-724's condition is global, so a deadlocked test is not detected while another test runs or waits to start, and D-724 does not say which tests fail. Per-test detection over the test's accounting domain (D-691), failing only that test, is proposed. The spec marks it OQ-13-8.
- The optimizations of D-731 each need an entry with benchmark data.
- User external types' drop functions and `@blocking` FFI users arrive with OQ-P9 in M3 (D-647, D-703, D-683).
- M6: Windows fibers, the Win64 and Windows aarch64 switches, and Windows's commit charge for stack regions; strict-overcommit hosts may be revisited with it.
- Tree-sitter: no grammar change. `@std_context` is an attribute name, which the grammar accepts generically; `cargo xtask cross-check` gains the `docs/stdlib` blocks (D-662). `../zed-cheby` needs nothing.

## 2026-10-12: M2 checkpoint 1

Goal: record the user's answers at M2's checkpoint 1: the unwinding backend, the decisions the slice's packages proposed, and the work that follows the checkpoint.

- **D-733 Unwinding by return-flag propagation**: On native targets, a panicking or cancelled fiber unwinds its stack and releases each frame's references by return-flag propagation, on D-698's MIR pass and cleanup pads, with D-699's catch points and D-697's Rust guards. A Cheby frame that unwinds runs its cleanup pad and returns 0. A caller tests the result of each Cheby call that may unwind, and after a C call that may unwind it loads the flag at `ctx+12` (D-685), except after `cheby_rt_alloc` and the boxing functions, whose result it tests. A guard sets the flag and returns a sentinel. Package U4 makes this backend the default, merges it with U3's runtime and deletes the `try_call` prototype, including its exit record at FCB offsets 16 and 24 (D-763) and its exit stubs `cb$exit$…` (D-764). Settles D-696. Why: the user chose it from D-702's measurement, taken at `a1ae525` on an Apple M3. Against the baseline without unwinding, the flag costs 14.1% and 14.3% of build throughput on the 100k and 1M shapes, 2.1% of run time on the geometric mean of the RC group against the same build without unwind edges (+1.7%, +3.3% and +1.5% in three rounds, with record_updates at +4.9% and list_pipeline at +3.2%), and 19% more code. `try_call` costs 18.3% and 18.1% of throughput, 17.5% of run time and 34.1% more code. With a value live across the call, a recursion reaches about 3.7 times fewer frames under `try_call`, because Cranelift's `try_call` to a `Tail` callee clobbers every register. `try_call`'s own code has 18 `unsafe` blocks and assembly, and it would need resume code for each Windows target in M6 and its tables serialized with M3's cached code; the flag has no architecture-specific code. Both passed the whole M1 suite. `try_call`, D-077's mechanism, was rejected for these costs. Supersedes D-077. → [ADR-0061](../adr/0061-return-flag-unwinding.md)
- **D-734 Unboxed-result entry points under the return flag**: A call of an unboxed-result entry point, which release builds may add (D-453), tests the flag at `ctx+12` after the call instead of its result, because an unboxed result can legitimately be 0. A call of the uniform-word entry keeps the result test, since a uniform word is never 0. Why: D-733 reads a Cheby call's result of 0 as an unwinding, which holds only for uniform words, and D-453 allows the unboxed entry points without saying how they signal one. Refines D-453.
- **D-735 The compile cost of unwinding**: Both backends missed D-702's 3% compile reference, and the user accepted D-733's backend anyway. On `bench-compile`'s 100k and 1M shapes, the MIR unwind pass alone costs 3.4% to 4.0% of build throughput against the same binary with the pass skipped. With the flag's code generation the cost is 14.1% and 14.3% against the commit before the pass, and `try_call`'s was about 18%. A package after U4 reduces the cost toward a target, with three levers: one walk for the pass and its `Stage::Unwound` validation, a table of kernels that never raise, which removes the unwind edges of calls to them, and cheaper pads. D-702's 3% stays the reference. Why: the front end's rate is the same in every configuration, so the whole cost is the pass and the code its edges generate, and each lever removes work that the pass or the flag adds. The choice of backend did not wait for the reduction, because `try_call` cost more on every measure. Refines D-702.
- **D-736 `let _ = x` is a use of `x`**: `let _ = x`, where `x` names a counted value, is a use of `x`, so `x` is released at that statement if it is the last use. `let _ = e` for any other expression is unchanged. Why: D-205 releases a binding right after its last use, and programs and std rely on `let _ = rx` to keep a handle alive to that point; without this the `Receiver` was released at an earlier destructuring and its channel closed early. Refines D-205.
- **D-737 Preemption when another fiber waits**: The monitor requests a yield from a fiber that has run 10 ms only when another fiber waits in a run queue; a lone fiber is not preempted (the debug migration stress mode preempts it too). A fiber taken from a worker's runnext slot continues the time slice of the fiber that woke it, so a chain of hand-offs through runnext counts as one fiber and yields after one quantum. Why: with nothing waiting, a yield resumes the same fiber, perhaps on another worker away from its warm allocator caches, and no program can tell the difference; without the inherited slice a capacity-0 ping-pong starved every other fiber on its worker, which §10.1 allows only on JS (D-185, D-232). Refines D-688.
- **D-738 Budget check before helpers that take owned arguments**: During constant evaluation with a memory budget, `ListPush`, `ListAppend` and `ListSliceOwned` first check the budget against an upper bound of what the update allocates (for a push onto a list of height h, 3h + 4 nodes and a root), then take their arguments over. Running out of budget then raises while the helper can still release its arguments whole, and the limit (E0076) may stop an evaluation up to that bound earlier than the update's own requests would. Why: D-698 requires a helper that raises to release its owned arguments, and raising halfway through an in-place structural update would leak the structure in flight. Refines D-617, D-698.
- **D-739 Pointers to the fiber's own control block**: D-686's rule covers thread state, not the running fiber's own control block. When a fiber becomes current on a thread, the runtime records that thread's allocator block-cache address in the fiber's control block, and clears it in the fiber it replaces. Allocation paths that already have their fiber read the cache through that record instead of a thread-local accessor, and never suspend while they use it. Debug builds assert that the fiber is current on the reading thread. A scratch buffer records the control block of the fiber it first grew on and keeps it for its whole life, across switches and migrations. Why: every thread-local access is an out-of-line call (D-686). The block-cache lookup cost tree_inserts about 6%, and looking up a scratch buffer's fiber at every growth cost map_updates about 7%. The cache record is rewritten at every switch-in, so D-686's hazard, a thread-local address reused after a migration, cannot arise, and a buffer lives in its fiber's frames, so the block it names is always live and right. Refines D-686.
- **D-740 Syntax of `CHEBY_THREADS`**: `CHEBY_THREADS` is ASCII decimal digits only, at least 1. Leading zeros are allowed; a sign and surrounding whitespace are not. A number too large for the machine word is valid and clamps to 1024. Why: D-658 says "a decimal whole number" without the lexical details, and a program setting is read exactly as written, unlike the harness's own variables, which trim whitespace. Refines D-658.
- **D-741 Pending spec tests of later milestones**: An entry of the pending spec-test list whose milestone is later than the one in effect also accepts its test as absent. An entry is an error when its milestone has ended, when its test is present in `std/`, when it names no spec test of a shipped module, or when it repeats another entry. Why: D-288 expects a later milestone's tests to fail, and D-728 exists so that a module can ship before all of its behavior lands.
